I’m not trying to make a big deal out of it, but it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried a lot of the time, even when there isn’t anything specific happening to cause it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to act like it, but I guess I do. I know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean it that way. It’s just kind of there, even when nothing’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep coming into your mind, even when you don’t want them there—like something you have to keep thinking about or doing to feel better?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’m trying to do anything weird to make it stop, if that’s what you mean. Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably want to admit.

I wish I could say it just comes and goes, but… it’s kind of there most of the time.

Yeah… most days, I’d say.

I’ll say I’ve got another task or that someone else is better for it. And I don’t really like talking about that day either, because once it gets started in my head, it’s like I can’t shut it off.\n\nI’m not trying to dodge work or anything… I just don’t always know how to keep it from taking over.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened that you keep flashing back to—was there a specific event or moment that’s been stuck in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, there’s a moment. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been getting way too jumpy. I know everybody gets startled sometimes, but for me it’s like if a breaker snaps, or a tool drops, or somebody comes up behind me at work, I just about jump out of my skin. My heart keeps pounding after, even when everyone else has already moved on. I’m not trying to make a big deal out of it, but it’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried a lot of the time, even when there isn’t anything specific happening to cause it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to act like it, but I guess I do. It’s not always “worried” like there’s one thing happening right then. It’s more like I walk around mad and guilty most of the time, like I failed that kid… and like everybody can see it on me. I know that probably sounds dramatic. I don’t mean it that way. It’s just kind of there, even when nothing’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep coming into your mind, even when you don’t want them there—like something you have to keep thinking about or doing to feel better?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’m trying to do anything weird to make it stop, if that’s what you mean. But thoughts, yeah. Or… flashes, I guess.\n\nI can be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck, and out of nowhere I’ll see that flash again, or hear the trainee yelling. It’s not like I sit there trying to think about it. It just hits me, and for a second it kind of knocks me sideways, like I’m right back in it. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, it comes in when I don’t want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of that moment or make you feel like you might have another one of those flashes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably want to admit.\n\nI’ll make excuses sometimes so I don’t have to be near certain panels or breakers, or certain job sites that feel too close to what happened. I’ll say I’ve got another task or that someone else is better for it. And I don’t really like talking about that day either, because once it gets started in my head, it’s like I can’t shut it off.\n\nI’m not trying to dodge work or anything… I just don’t always know how to keep it from taking over.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened that you keep flashing back to—was there a specific event or moment that’s been stuck in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, there’s a moment. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but it’s stuck there.\n\nIt’ll just come out of nowhere. Like I can be doing something normal, loading tools in the truck or getting ready for the day, and suddenly I’ll see that flash in my head again. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling. And it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it… it just hits me.\n\nFor a second it kind of knocks me sideways, like I’m back there and it’s happening again. Then I try to shake it off and keep moving, you know? I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened during that moment you keep flashing back to? Was it something that felt really threatening or dangerous at the time?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how much detail you need. It did feel dangerous in the moment, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it scared me pretty bad.\n\nThere was this sudden flash and yelling, and everything felt like it happened all at once. For a second I didn’t know if someone was seriously hurt or if it was going to get worse. I just remember my body kind of locking up and my head going blank.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right. It’s hard to explain without… getting back into it too much.\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you noticed that you often feel angry, guilty, or upset, even when you’re trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to be like that. I don’t want to come off angry or like I’m blaming anybody.\n\nBut yeah, I walk around mad a lot. And guilty. Like I should’ve done something different, or I failed that kid somehow. It’s kind of just sitting on me all the time, even when I’m trying to think about work or normal stuff.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make much sense, but it feels like everybody can see it on me. Like I’m carrying it around and I’m not hiding it as well as I think. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you found yourself being extra watchful or on guard, like you’re always waiting for something dangerous to happen again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe not like that exactly. I mean, I’m careful at work anyway, you kind of have to be, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal and what’s me being weird about it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking something bad is definitely about to happen all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Sometimes I do catch myself checking things more than I need to, but… I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I’m just not sure I’d call it “always on guard.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you noticed that these feelings of anger and guilt have been constant or do they come and go depending on what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it just comes and goes, but… it’s kind of there most of the time.\n\nSome days I can keep busy enough that it’s not right in my face, but it doesn’t really leave. I walk around mad, and then I feel guilty for being mad. And that thought about failing that kid… it just stays with me.\n\nI try not to show it, because I don’t want people thinking I’m falling apart or anything, but it feels like everybody can see it on me anyway. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily startled by small things, like noises or someone suddenly walking by, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I hate to make it sound dramatic, but yes.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. At work, if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, I just about jump out of my skin. Or if somebody walks up behind me without me hearing them, I react like… way bigger than I should.\n\nAnd then everybody else moves on, like it was nothing, but my heart’s still pounding for a while after. I try to play it off, like “yeah, you got me,” but it’s embarrassing. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of anger and guilt are present most days, even when nothing specific is happening to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit there trying to think about it. It just hits me, and for a second it kind of knocks me sideways, like I’m right back in it. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, it comes in when I don’t want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of that moment or make you feel like you might have another one of those flashes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably want to admit.\n\nI’ll make excuses sometimes so I don’t have to be near certain panels or breakers, or certain job sites that feel too close to what happened. I’ll say I’ve got another task or that someone else is better for it. And I don’t really like talking about that day either, because once it gets started in my head, it’s like I can’t shut it off.\n\nI’m not trying to dodge work or anything… I just don’t always know how to keep it from taking over.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened that you keep flashing back to—was there a specific event or moment that’s been stuck in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, there’s a moment. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but it’s stuck there.\n\nIt’ll just come out of nowhere. Like I can be doing something normal, loading tools in the truck or getting ready for the day, and suddenly I’ll see that flash in my head again. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling. Then I try to shake it off and keep moving, you know? I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happened during that moment you keep flashing back to? Was it something that felt really threatening or dangerous at the time?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how much detail you need. It did feel dangerous in the moment, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it scared me pretty bad.\n\nThere was this sudden flash and yelling, and everything felt like it happened all at once. For a second I didn’t know if someone was seriously hurt or if it was going to get worse. I just remember my body kind of locking up and my head going blank.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right. It’s hard to explain without… getting back into it too much.\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you noticed that you often feel angry, guilty, or upset, even when you’re trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to be like that. I don’t want to come off angry or like I’m blaming anybody.\n\nBut yeah, I walk around mad a lot. And guilty. Like I should’ve done something different, or I failed that kid somehow. It’s kind of just sitting on me all the time, even when I’m trying to think about work or normal stuff.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make much sense, but it feels like everybody can see it on me. Like I’m carrying it around and I’m not hiding it as well as I think. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you found yourself being extra watchful or on guard, like you’re always waiting for something dangerous to happen again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe not like that exactly. I mean, I’m careful at work anyway, you kind of have to be, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s normal and what’s me being weird about it.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m walking around thinking something bad is definitely about to happen all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Sometimes I do catch myself checking things more than I need to, but… I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I’m just not sure I’d call it “always on guard.” Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Since that moment happened, have you noticed that these feelings of anger and guilt have been constant or do they come and go depending on what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it just comes and goes, but… it’s kind of there most of the time.\n\nSome days I can keep busy enough that it’s not right in my face, but it doesn’t really leave. I walk around mad, and then I feel guilty for being mad. And that thought about failing that kid… it just stays with me.\n\nI try not to show it, because I don’t want people thinking I’m falling apart or anything, but it feels like everybody can see it on me anyway. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily startled by small things, like noises or someone suddenly walking by, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I hate to make it sound dramatic, but yes.\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. At work, if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, I just about jump out of my skin. Or if somebody walks up behind me without me hearing them, I react like… way bigger than I should.\n\nAnd then everybody else moves on, like it was nothing, but my heart’s still pounding for a while after. I try to play it off, like “yeah, you got me,” but it’s embarrassing. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of anger and guilt are present most days, even when nothing specific is happening to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just angry at everybody all day, but it’s there most days.\n\nEven when nothing’s really happening, I’ll still feel mad, and then guilty right after. Like I keep coming back to the thought that I failed that kid somehow. And I know people probably aren’t actually staring at me or anything, but it feels like everybody can see it on me.\n\nIs that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of anger and guilt are present most days, even when nothing specific is happening to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nI mean, I try to keep it together and not act like I’m walking around mad all the time, but inside it’s kind of there. The anger, and then the guilt right behind it. I keep thinking I failed that kid somehow, and it feels like everybody can see that on me, even if they probably can’t.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the right way to explain it, but… it doesn’t really go away much.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of anger and guilt are present most days, even when nothing specific is happening to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I’m probably repeating myself, but yes. It’s not just when something happens.\n\nI kind of walk around with it most days — mad, and then guilty on top of it. Like I failed that kid, and somehow everybody can see that on me, even when nobody’s saying anything.\n\nI try not to show it, because I don’t want to be that guy, but… it’s there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble relaxing, being constantly alert, or feeling tense, even in situations where there's no real danger?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one.\n\nI mean… I don’t think I’m in danger or anything most of the time. I wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I guess I have days where I’m kind of wound up, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by constantly alert or tense.\n\nMaybe I just don’t notice it as much? What would that look like, exactly?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of anger and guilt are present most days, even when nothing specific is happening to trigger them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I’d say most days.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m out of control or anything, because I’m not trying to be that way. But inside, I walk around mad and guilty a lot. Like I failed that kid, and like people can see it on me somehow.\n\nI know that might not make total sense, but that’s what it feels like."
